Yeah… thank you for seeing me. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, and I know people probably think I’m over-focusing on it, but I’ve been having a hard time letting go of some things around my father’s death.

I mean… I wouldn’t say it’s happened a lot, or that I’m the kind of person who just decides things and won’t listen. I try to be fair.

I don’t want to make it sound like I think everyone is sitting in a room planning it together. That sounds… extreme, and I know how it might come across.

But I do think there’s something being kept from me. When I compare the hospital records with the donation emails, the timestamps don’t sit right with me, and there are attachments missing. And then when certain people in the community avoid answering pretty basic questions, it doesn’t feel like simple discomfort. Maybe some of them don’t know the full story, sure. But overall, to me, it feels like someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.

I’m trying to say that carefully, because I know it probably sounds suspicious. But that is honestly how it feels to me.

But nothing that felt… major, or impossible, or that other people had to talk me out of.\n\nI don’t know if that’s a useful answer. I’m trying not to dodge the question. I just can’t think of another example.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people who are avoiding your questions about your father’s death are part of a coordinated effort to hide the truth, or do you think they might be avoiding the topic for other reasons like discomfort or not knowing the answers?\n\nPatient: I don’t want to make it sound like I think everyone is sitting in a room planning it together. That sounds… extreme, and I know how it might come across.\n\nBut I do think there’s something being kept from me. When I compare the hospital records with the donation emails, the timestamps don’t sit right with me, and there are attachments missing. And then when certain people in the community avoid answering pretty basic questions, it doesn’t feel like simple discomfort. Maybe some of them don’t know the full story, sure. But overall, to me, it feels like someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nI’m trying to say that carefully, because I know it probably sounds suspicious. But that is honestly how it feels to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I understand that. But when I look at the timestamps myself, and the missing attachments, and then certain people in the community won’t really answer my questions… it still feels obvious to me that something is being hidden.\n\nI’m not trying to ignore proof just to be stubborn. I just can’t make myself believe the version everyone else accepts.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel that your belief about someone hiding the truth regarding your father’s death is something you just know in the same way you know your own name, even though there’s no clear evidence that supports it?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I wouldn’t put it quite as strongly as knowing my own name… that sounds a little too absolute, and I don’t want to sound unreasonable.

I understand that. But when I look at the timestamps myself, and the missing attachments, and then certain people in the community won’t really answer my questions… it still feels obvious to me that something is being hidden.\n\nI’m not trying to ignore proof just to be stubborn. Everyone keeps telling me the hospital records and donation emails line up, and I hear that. I understand that’s what they’re saying. But when I compare the timestamps, the missing attachments, and the way certain people in the community avoid my questions, it just… it’s obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nI know that may not count as clear evidence to you. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… I don’t want to say “nothing could change my mind,” because that sounds like I’m being closed off, and I don’t want to come across that way.

But honestly, it has felt very certain. I know everyone keeps telling me the hospital records and donation emails all line up, and I understand that’s supposed to explain it. But when I compare the timestamps, the missing attachments, and the way certain people in the community avoid my questions, it feels obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.

So… yes, I guess there have been times where explanations from other people didn’t really move it for me. I’m trying to be reasonable about it, but that feeling doesn’t go away.

I’m trying to be reasonable about it, but that feeling doesn’t go away.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you recall a time when you had a belief that you felt absolutely certain about, even though you knew it contradicted what almost everyone else believed and there was no solid proof to support it?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 34 / computer_systems_analyst / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I know everyone keeps telling me the hospital records and donation emails all line up, but when I compare the timestamps, missing attachments, and the way certain community members avoid my questions, it’s obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I can recall that. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but with my father’s death, I’ve had that feeling.

I know almost everyone around me believes the official version — that the hospital records and the donation emails all line up and explain everything. And I know there isn’t, like, one solid piece of proof I can hand you that proves otherwise.

But when I look at the timestamps, the missing attachments, and the way some people in the community avoid answering me directly, it feels… obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’d like to think I could accept it. I mean, if it was truly undeniable, I don’t want to be the kind of person who just ignores facts. I’m trying to be reasonable about this.

But… if I’m being completely honest, I think part of me would still wonder whether I was being shown the whole picture. Because everyone already tells me the hospital records and donation emails line up, and I understand that’s supposed to settle it. It’s just that when I look at the timestamps, the missing attachments, and how certain people in the community avoid my questions, it still feels obvious to me that something was hidden about what really happened around my father’s death.

So I guess I could say I’d try to accept the evidence… but I’m not sure the feeling would go away right away. I hope that makes sense.

I’m trying to be reasonable about it, but that feeling doesn’t go away.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you had a belief that you felt absolutely certain about, even though you knew it contradicted what almost everyone else believed and there was no solid proof to support it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I can recall that. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but with my father’s death, I’ve had that feeling.\n\nI know almost everyone around me believes the official version — that the hospital records and the donation emails all line up and explain everything. And I know there isn’t, like, one solid piece of proof I can hand you that proves otherwise.\n\nBut when I look at the timestamps, the missing attachments, and the way some people in the community avoid answering me directly, it feels… obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened. I realize that may not sound convincing from the outside. I’m not trying to be difficult. It’s just hard for me to let go of it.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how you would react if someone were to provide you with strong, undeniable evidence that your concerns about your father’s death were unfounded? Would you be able to accept that evidence, or would you still feel certain that something was hidden?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think I could accept it. I mean, if it was truly undeniable, I don’t want to be the kind of person who just ignores facts. I’m trying to be reasonable about this.\n\nBut… if I’m being completely honest, I think part of me would still wonder whether I was being shown the whole picture. Because everyone already tells me the hospital records and donation emails line up, and I understand that’s supposed to settle it. It’s just that when I look at the timestamps, the missing attachments, and how certain people in the community avoid my questions, it still feels obvious to me that something was hidden about what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nSo I guess I could say I’d try to accept the evidence… but I’m not sure the feeling would go away right away. I hope that makes sense.\n\n[Last Question]\nSo, if I understand correctly, despite others showing you hospital records and emails that support the explanation of your father's death, you still feel certain that something is being hidden. Has this belief remained completely unshaken, even when faced with evidence that contradicts it?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 34 / computer_systems_analyst / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I know everyone keeps telling me the hospital records and donation emails all line up, but when I compare the timestamps, missing attachments, and the way certain community members avoid my questions, it’s obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wouldn’t say completely unshaken, because I don’t want to sound like I’m refusing to listen. I do hear what people are saying, and I know the records and emails are supposed to make sense of it.

But… yes, the belief is still there. Even with all that, when I compare the timestamps and notice the missing attachments, and then certain people in the community avoid my questions, it still feels obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.

I know that may not be the answer you’re hoping for, but that’s honestly how it feels to me.

Even with all that, when I compare the timestamps and notice the missing attachments, and then certain people in the community avoid my questions, it still feels obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nI know that may not be the answer you’re hoping for, but that’s honestly how it feels to me.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say your belief that someone is hiding the truth about your father's death is something you hold as strongly as other important truths in your life, even though others don't share that belief and have shown you evidence that contradicts it?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think that’s fair to say, though I don’t love how it sounds.

When I compare the hospital records with the donation emails, the timestamps don’t sit right with me, and there are attachments missing. And then when certain people in the community avoid answering pretty basic questions, it doesn’t feel like simple discomfort. Maybe some of them don’t know the full story, sure. But overall, to me, it feels like someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nI’m trying to say that carefully, because I know it probably sounds suspicious. But that is honestly how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief that you knew was definitely true, even when people showed you clear proof that it wasn’t?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, if I’m being honest. I just can’t make myself believe the version everyone else accepts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your belief about someone hiding the truth regarding your father’s death is something you just know in the same way you know your own name, even though there’s no clear evidence that supports it?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t put it quite as strongly as knowing my own name… that sounds a little too absolute, and I don’t want to sound unreasonable.\n\nBut yes, it does feel very clear to me. Everyone keeps telling me the hospital records and donation emails line up, and I hear that. I understand that’s what they’re saying. But when I compare the timestamps, the missing attachments, and the way certain people in the community avoid my questions, it just… it’s obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nI know that may not count as clear evidence to you. I’m trying to be fair about that. But to me, it doesn’t feel like just a guess.\n\nDoctor: Has your feeling that someone is hiding the truth about your father's death ever felt so certain that no amount of evidence or explanation from others could change your mind?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t want to say “nothing could change my mind,” because that sounds like I’m being closed off, and I don’t want to come across that way.\n\nBut honestly, it has felt very certain. I mean… “absolute truth” sounds a little strong, and I don’t want to come across like I’m impossible to reason with.

But yes, if I’m being honest, I do feel very certain about it. But when I compare the timestamps, and some attachments that seem to be missing, and then the way certain people in the community avoid answering my questions… it just feels obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened.\n\nI know that may sound a little intense. I just thought maybe talking to someone would help me sort out whether I’m looking at it correctly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt certain about something in the past that others told you wasn’t true, even though you still believed it was?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say it’s happened a lot, or that I’m the kind of person who just decides things and won’t listen. I try to be fair.\n\nBut with my father’s death, yes. People keep telling me the hospital records and the donation emails all line up, and maybe on the surface they do. But when I look at the timestamps, and there are attachments missing, and then certain people in the community kind of dodge my questions… I don’t know how else to read that. It feels obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened.\n\nI know that probably sounds bad, or like I’m being stubborn. I’m not trying to be. I just can’t honestly say I believe their explanation.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been absolutely certain about something that turned out not to be true, even though at the time you really believed it was? But when I compare the timestamps, and the missing attachments, and then the way some people in the community avoid my questions… it just feels obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened.\n\nI realize that might be exactly the kind of thing you’re asking about. I’m not trying to be difficult or insist I’m right no matter what. I just… I haven’t been able to accept the version everyone else seems comfortable with.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time, unrelated to your father’s death, when you were completely sure something was true, but later found out it definitely wasn’t?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can think of one, honestly. Not in the way you mean—where I was completely sure and then it was clearly proven wrong.\n\nI mean, I’ve been wrong about normal things before, of course—misremembering a meeting time, assuming someone meant something in an email and then realizing I misread it. I just can’t think of another example.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people who are avoiding your questions about your father’s death are part of a coordinated effort to hide the truth, or do you think they might be avoiding the topic for other reasons like discomfort or not knowing the answers?\n\nPatient: I don’t want to make it sound like I think everyone is sitting in a room planning it together. That sounds… extreme, and I know how it might come across.\n\nBut I do think there’s something being kept from me. When I compare the hospital records with the donation emails, the timestamps don’t sit right with me, and there are attachments missing. And then when certain people in the community avoid answering pretty basic questions, it doesn’t feel like simple discomfort. Maybe some of them don’t know the full story, sure. But overall, to me, it feels like someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nI’m trying to say that carefully, because I know it probably sounds suspicious. But that is honestly how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief that you knew was definitely true, even when people showed you clear proof that it wasn’t?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, if I’m being honest. Not in a dramatic way, but with my father’s death, people have shown me the hospital records and the donation emails and said they all line up.\n\nAnd I know that should probably be enough. I understand that. But when I look at the timestamps myself, and the missing attachments, and then certain people in the community won’t really answer my questions… it still feels obvious to me that something is being hidden.\n\nI’m not trying to ignore proof just to be stubborn. I just can’t make myself believe the version everyone else accepts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your belief about someone hiding the truth regarding your father’s death is something you just know in the same way you know your own name, even though there’s no clear evidence that supports it?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t put it quite as strongly as knowing my own name… that sounds a little too absolute, and I don’t want to sound unreasonable.\n\nBut yes, it does feel very clear to me. Everyone keeps telling me the hospital records and donation emails line up, and I hear that. I understand that’s what they’re saying. But when I compare the timestamps, the missing attachments, and the way certain people in the community avoid my questions, it just… it’s obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nI know that may not count as clear evidence to you. I’m trying to be fair about that. But to me, it doesn’t feel like just a guess.\n\nDoctor: Has your feeling that someone is hiding the truth about your father's death ever felt so certain that no amount of evidence or explanation from others could change your mind?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t want to say “nothing could change my mind,” because that sounds like I’m being closed off, and I don’t want to come across that way.\n\nBut honestly, it has felt very certain. I know everyone keeps telling me the hospital records and donation emails all line up, and I understand that’s supposed to explain it. But when I compare the timestamps, the missing attachments, and the way certain people in the community avoid my questions, it feels obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nSo… yes, I guess there have been times where explanations from other people didn’t really move it for me. I’m trying to be reasonable about it, but that feeling doesn’t go away.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you had a belief that you felt absolutely certain about, even though you knew it contradicted what almost everyone else believed and there was no solid proof to support it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I can recall that. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but with my father’s death, I’ve had that feeling.\n\nI know almost everyone around me believes the official version — that the hospital records and the donation emails all line up and explain everything. I realize that may not sound convincing from the outside. I’m not trying to be difficult. It’s just hard for me to let go of it.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how you would react if someone were to provide you with strong, undeniable evidence that your concerns about your father’s death were unfounded? Would you be able to accept that evidence, or would you still feel certain that something was hidden?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think I could accept it. I mean, if it was truly undeniable, I don’t want to be the kind of person who just ignores facts. I’m trying to be reasonable about this.\n\nBut… if I’m being completely honest, I think part of me would still wonder whether I was being shown the whole picture. I understand that. I just can’t make myself believe the version everyone else accepts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your belief about someone hiding the truth regarding your father’s death is something you just know in the same way you know your own name, even though there’s no clear evidence that supports it?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t put it quite as strongly as knowing my own name… that sounds a little too absolute, and I don’t want to sound unreasonable.\n\nBut yes, it does feel very clear to me. Everyone keeps telling me the hospital records and donation emails line up, and I hear that. I understand that’s what they’re saying. But when I compare the timestamps, the missing attachments, and the way certain people in the community avoid my questions, it just… it’s obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nI know that may not count as clear evidence to you. I’m trying to be fair about that. But to me, it doesn’t feel like just a guess.\n\nDoctor: Has your feeling that someone is hiding the truth about your father's death ever felt so certain that no amount of evidence or explanation from others could change your mind?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t want to say “nothing could change my mind,” because that sounds like I’m being closed off, and I don’t want to come across that way.\n\nBut honestly, it has felt very certain. I know everyone keeps telling me the hospital records and the donation emails line up, and maybe they do if you just look at them quickly. But when I compare the timestamps, and the missing attachments, and then the way some people in the community avoid my questions… it just feels obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened.\n\nI realize that might be exactly the kind of thing you’re asking about. I’m not trying to be difficult or insist I’m right no matter what. I just… I haven’t been able to accept the version everyone else seems comfortable with.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time, unrelated to your father’s death, when you were completely sure something was true, but later found out it definitely wasn’t?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can think of one, honestly. Not in the way you mean—where I was completely sure and then it was clearly proven wrong.\n\nI mean, I’ve been wrong about normal things before, of course—misremembering a meeting time, assuming someone meant something in an email and then realizing I misread it. But nothing that felt… major, or impossible, or that other people had to talk me out of.\n\nI don’t know if that’s a useful answer. I’m trying not to dodge the question. I just can’t think of another example.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the people who are avoiding your questions about your father’s death are part of a coordinated effort to hide the truth, or do you think they might be avoiding the topic for other reasons like discomfort or not knowing the answers?\n\nPatient: I don’t want to make it sound like I think everyone is sitting in a room planning it together. That sounds… extreme, and I know how it might come across.\n\nBut I do think there’s something being kept from me. When I compare the hospital records with the donation emails, the timestamps don’t sit right with me, and there are attachments missing. And then when certain people in the community avoid answering pretty basic questions, it doesn’t feel like simple discomfort. Maybe some of them don’t know the full story, sure. But overall, to me, it feels like someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nI’m trying to say that carefully, because I know it probably sounds suspicious. But that is honestly how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief that you knew was definitely true, even when people showed you clear proof that it wasn’t?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, if I’m being honest. Not in a dramatic way, but with my father’s death, people have shown me the hospital records and the donation emails and said they all line up.\n\nAnd I know that should probably be enough. I understand that. But when I look at the timestamps myself, and the missing attachments, and then certain people in the community won’t really answer my questions… it still feels obvious to me that something is being hidden.\n\nI’m not trying to ignore proof just to be stubborn. I just can’t make myself believe the version everyone else accepts.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your belief about someone hiding the truth regarding your father’s death is something you just know in the same way you know your own name, even though there’s no clear evidence that supports it?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t put it quite as strongly as knowing my own name… that sounds a little too absolute, and I don’t want to sound unreasonable.\n\nBut yes, it does feel very clear to me. Everyone keeps telling me the hospital records and donation emails line up, and I hear that. I understand that’s what they’re saying. But when I compare the timestamps, the missing attachments, and the way certain people in the community avoid my questions, it just… it’s obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nI know that may not count as clear evidence to you. I’m trying to be fair about that. But to me, it doesn’t feel like just a guess.\n\nDoctor: Has your feeling that someone is hiding the truth about your father's death ever felt so certain that no amount of evidence or explanation from others could change your mind?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t want to say “nothing could change my mind,” because that sounds like I’m being closed off, and I don’t want to come across that way.\n\nBut honestly, it has felt very certain. I know everyone keeps telling me the hospital records and donation emails all line up, and I understand that’s supposed to explain it. But when I compare the timestamps, the missing attachments, and the way certain people in the community avoid my questions, it feels obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nSo… yes, I guess there have been times where explanations from other people didn’t really move it for me. I’m trying to be reasonable about it, but that feeling doesn’t go away.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time when you had a belief that you felt absolutely certain about, even though you knew it contradicted what almost everyone else believed and there was no solid proof to support it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I can recall that. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it is, but with my father’s death, I’ve had that feeling.\n\nI know almost everyone around me believes the official version — that the hospital records and the donation emails all line up and explain everything. And I know there isn’t, like, one solid piece of proof I can hand you that proves otherwise.\n\nBut when I look at the timestamps, the missing attachments, and the way some people in the community avoid answering me directly, it feels… obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened. I realize that may not sound convincing from the outside. I’m not trying to be difficult. It’s just hard for me to let go of it.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how you would react if someone were to provide you with strong, undeniable evidence that your concerns about your father’s death were unfounded? Would you be able to accept that evidence, or would you still feel certain that something was hidden?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think I could accept it. I mean, if it was truly undeniable, I don’t want to be the kind of person who just ignores facts. I’m trying to be reasonable about this.\n\nBut… if I’m being completely honest, I think part of me would still wonder whether I was being shown the whole picture. Because everyone already tells me the hospital records and donation emails line up, and I understand that’s supposed to settle it. It’s just that when I look at the timestamps, the missing attachments, and how certain people in the community avoid my questions, it still feels obvious to me that something was hidden about what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nSo I guess I could say I’d try to accept the evidence… but I’m not sure the feeling would go away right away. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: So, if I understand correctly, despite others showing you hospital records and emails that support the explanation of your father's death, you still feel certain that something is being hidden. Has this belief remained completely unshaken, even when faced with evidence that contradicts it?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say completely unshaken, because I don’t want to sound like I’m refusing to listen. I do hear what people are saying, and I know the records and emails are supposed to make sense of it.\n\nBut… yes, the belief is still there. Even with all that, when I compare the timestamps and notice the missing attachments, and then certain people in the community avoid my questions, it still feels obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nI know that may not be the answer you’re hoping for, but that’s honestly how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your belief that someone is hiding the truth about your father's death is something you hold as strongly as other important truths in your life, even though others don't share that belief and have shown you evidence that contradicts it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say, though I don’t love how it sounds.\n\nI know everyone keeps telling me the hospital records and the donation emails all line up, and I understand that other people see that as enough. But when I compare the timestamps, the missing attachments, and the way certain community members avoid my questions, it feels obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nI’m not trying to be stubborn or dramatic. It’s just… yes, I hold it pretty strongly. Maybe stronger than people think I should.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your belief about someone hiding the truth regarding your father's death is something you are as certain about as any other absolute truth in your life, even though others strongly disagree and have shown you evidence that contradicts it?\n\nPatient: I mean… “absolute truth” sounds a little strong, and I don’t want to come across like I’m impossible to reason with.\n\nBut yes, if I’m being honest, I do feel very certain about it. I know everyone keeps telling me the hospital records and the donation emails line up, and I understand that’s supposed to answer things. But when I compare the timestamps, the missing attachments, and the way certain people in the community avoid my questions, it just feels obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\n\nI wish I could say it felt less certain than that, because I know that would probably sound better. But that’s where I am.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a time when you believed something so strongly that, even after seeing clear proof that it wasn’t true, you still couldn’t shake the belief?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes.
